So, yeah, it wasn’t just something that suddenly started with work.\n\nDoctor: When you notice yourself needing to get up and move during meetings, is it usually accompanied by a lot of worried or anxious thoughts running through your mind, or does it happen even when you're not particularly worried about anything?\n\nPatient: It happens even when I’m not especially worried. Like, the big thing is those long sales strategy meetings—I know I’m supposed to stay seated, but I’ll just feel like I have to get up, refill my water, or I’ll wander over by the printer and kind of pace for a minute. Sometimes I’m thinking about the meeting, sometimes I’m not thinking about much at all. It’s not always, like, anxiety driving it.\n\n[Last Question]\nAside from the restlessness, do you currently notice problems like losing focus during tasks, forgetting things, or losing track of items you need for work?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 30 / advertising_sales_agent / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Sustained_Attention_Difficulty\nmanifestation: \"In longer strategy meetings or while reading a client brief, I start out focused, but after a few minutes I realize I have been staring at the same slide or paragraph and have no idea what the last point was.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared.   },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, focus is definitely an issue. Like in longer strategy meetings, I’ll start out really trying to follow along, and then a few minutes later I realize I’ve just been staring at the same slide and I don’t actually know what the last point was.

It happens when I’m reading client briefs too. I’ll be looking at a paragraph, and then I’m like, wait, did I read that? And I have to go back. Which is annoying because some of those briefs are already kind of dense and full of buzzwords, and then I start thinking about how we’re going to pitch it, or what the client really means by “brand authenticity,” and then I realize I’ve completely stopped absorbing the actual text.

At home, I’m honestly not sure I can give a clean example. I don’t want to overstate it.

The clearest version I notice is still with work stuff, like I’ll get almost all of a proposal done or update most of something in the CRM, and then somehow leave off the very last step—sending the final version, or logging the follow-up—even though I fully meant to finish it. So… at home, maybe it happens, but I’m having a harder time pinning down a specific example. The follow-through issue is definitely easier for me to see with work tasks.

your life outside of work—like at home, during conversations with friends or family, or when you're doing personal tasks?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I probably do sometimes, but I haven’t really tracked it outside of work the same way. Work is just where it’s most obvious because there are meetings and people notice.\n\nAt home it’s more like… I’ll start doing something and then end up doing three other little things, but I don’t know if that’s the same thing or just normal. And with friends, I try not to interrupt, though I’m sure I’ve done it. Sorry, I’m kind of thinking of random examples now. I guess I’d say I haven’t noticed it as clearly outside work.\n\nDoctor: Thinking specifically about home—like paying bills, managing chores, or following through on personal projects—do you find yourself losing focus, leaving tasks unfinished, or feeling restless in the same way you do at work?\n\nPatient: At home, I’m honestly not sure I can give a clean example. I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nThe clearest version I notice is still with work stuff, like I’ll get almost all of a proposal done or update most of something in the CRM, and then somehow leave off the very last step—sending the final version, or logging the follow-up—even though I fully meant to finish it. It’s not like I decided not to do it, it’s more like I mentally moved on too early.\n\nSo… at home, maybe it happens, but I’m having a harder time pinning down a specific example. The follow-through issue is definitely easier for me to see with work tasks.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me about a specific example of paying bills or handling personal paperwork at home—do you ever miss due dates, forget to finish the process, or lose track of the task partway through?\n\nPatient: With bills or personal paperwork, I’m trying to think of a really specific example and I’m blanking a little. I don’t want to say I’m constantly missing due dates if I’m not sure.\n\nThe thing that’s most like that, though, is at work—like I’ll get almost all the way through a proposal or a CRM update, and then somehow not do the final step. Like I’ll forget to send the final version, or I won’t log the follow-up even though that was the whole point. And it’s not that I didn’t care or decided to skip it, I just kind of mentally move on before it’s actually finished.\n\nAt home, I guess it might happen with paperwork too, but I’m having trouble pulling up a clean example right now.\n\n[Last Question]\nOutside of work, do you notice these issues affecting things like your social life or hobbies—for example, losing focus during conversations, forgetting plans, or feeling restless during activities you enjoy, like watching a movie or being at a social gathering?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
With social stuff, I’m not sure I notice it as much as at work. I mean, if I’m with friends I can usually follow along, unless it’s a loud place or something, but that feels kind of normal.

The clearer thing is when someone is talking directly to me at work—like my manager giving me feedback, or a client explaining what they want changed. I’ll be looking right at them, nodding, like I’m listening, and then a few seconds later I realize I missed a key part and I have to ask them to repeat it. Which is embarrassing, because it looks like I wasn’t paying attention even though I thought I was.

Outside work, maybe it happens in conversations too, but I can’t think of a super clean example right now.

But it’s been noticeable enough that I thought maybe I should talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed this kind of restlessness and difficulty sitting still since you were a child, or is this something that started more recently as an adult?\n\nPatient: No, it definitely goes back. I mean, I don’t remember thinking of it as “restlessness” back then, but in elementary school my teachers would write stuff like I was bright but rushed through things, talked out of turn, forgot homework… and my desk was always this disaster, like stuffed with half-finished papers. So, yeah, it wasn’t just something that suddenly started with work.\n\nDoctor: When you notice yourself needing to get up and move during meetings, is it usually accompanied by a lot of worried or anxious thoughts running through your mind, or does it happen even when you're not particularly worried about anything?\n\nPatient: It happens even when I’m not especially worried. Like, the big thing is those long sales strategy meetings—I know I’m supposed to stay seated, but I’ll just feel like I have to get up, refill my water, or I’ll wander over by the printer and kind of pace for a minute. Sometimes I’m thinking about the meeting, sometimes I’m not thinking about much at all. It’s not always, like, anxiety driving it.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the restlessness, do you currently notice problems like losing focus during tasks, forgetting things, or losing track of items you need for work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, focus is definitely an issue. I’ll be looking at a paragraph, and then I’m like, wait, did I read that? And I have to go back. conversation to add my thought, even when it probably could have waited.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: Onset prior to age 12 years\n  manifestation: I remember this starting way before high school—my elementary school teachers wrote that I was bright but rushed, talked out of turn, forgot homework, and had a desk stuffed with half-finished papers.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: At work, it has started showing up in performance reviews and client trust; one client complaint especially made me worry that I could damage accounts even though I care a lot about doing good work.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 2 years and 2 months\n  manifestation: This has been noticeably affecting me since I changed into advertising sales strategy about two years and two months ago.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 30 / advertising_sales_agent / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S011\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Thinking specifically about home—like paying bills, managing chores, or following through on I mean, if I’m with friends I can usually follow along, unless it’s a loud place or something, but that feels kind of normal.\n\nThe clearer thing is when someone is talking directly to me at work—like my manager giving me feedback, or a client explaining what they want changed. I’ll be looking right at them, nodding, like I’m listening, and then a few seconds later I realize I missed a key part and I have to ask them to repeat it. Which is embarrassing, because it looks like I wasn’t paying attention even though I thought I was.\n\nOutside work, maybe it happens in conversations too, but I can’t think of a super clean example right now.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you're driving, do you notice similar issues—like feeling restless, making impulsive decisions (such as cutting people off or changing lanes quickly), or losing focus and missing turns or exits?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Driving… I don’t know, I’m not sure I’d say that’s the main place I notice it. I don’t really have a clear example of cutting people off or anything like that.

The thing it reminds me of more is when I’m in longer strategy meetings, or even reading a client brief. I’ll start off focused, like I’m genuinely trying to follow it, and then a few minutes later I realize I’ve been staring at the same slide or the same paragraph and I don’t actually know what the last point was. Then I have to kind of backtrack mentally, or reread it.

So with driving, I’m not sure. Maybe it’s not as obvious there. But that “I was looking at it and somehow didn’t take it in” thing definitely happens with meetings and briefs.

I mean… not so much around the house that I can give you a perfect example. My partner has said I seem like I “leave things unfinished,” but when I think of the clearest version of that, it’s honestly more work stuff again.

Like I’ll do almost all of a proposal or a CRM update, and in my head it feels basically done, but then I leave off the last step—sending the final version, or logging the follow-up. And that’s the part that actually matters. I meant to finish it, I didn’t decide not to, I just sort of move on mentally before I close the loop.

At home it’s probably similar with little responsibilities, but I’m having trouble naming one clean example.

But it’s been noticeable enough that I thought maybe I should talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed this kind of restlessness and difficulty sitting still since you were a child, or is this something that started more recently as an adult?\n\nPatient: No, it definitely goes back. I’ll be looking at a paragraph, and then I’m like, wait, did I read that? And I have to go back. I’ll jump in with an answer before the person’s even finished the question—especially with clients or coworkers—because I think I know where they’re going with it. It’s not like I decided not to do it, it’s more like I mentally moved on too early.\n\nSo… at home, maybe it happens, but I’m having a harder time pinning down a specific example. The follow-through issue is definitely easier for me to see with work tasks.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me about a specific example of paying bills or handling personal paperwork at home—do you ever miss due dates, forget to finish the process, or lose track of the task partway through?\n\nPatient: With bills or personal paperwork, I’m trying to think of a really specific example and I’m blanking a little. I don’t want to say I’m constantly missing due dates if I’m not sure.\n\nThe thing that’s most like that, though, is at work—like I’ll get almost all the way through a proposal or a CRM update, and then somehow not do the final step. Like I’ll forget to send the final version, or I won’t log the follow-up even though that was the whole point. And it’s not that I didn’t care or decided to skip it, I just kind of mentally move on before it’s actually finished.\n\nAt home, I guess it might happen with paperwork too, but I’m having trouble pulling up a clean example right now.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work, do you notice these issues affecting things like your social life or hobbies—for example, losing focus during conversations, forgetting plans, or feeling restless during activities you enjoy, like watching a movie or being at a social gathering?\n\nPatient: With social stuff, I’m not sure I notice it as much as at work. I mean, if I’m with friends I can usually follow along, unless it’s a loud place or something, but that feels kind of normal.\n\nThe clearer thing is when someone is talking directly to me at work—like my manager giving me feedback, or a client explaining what they want changed. I’ll be looking right at them, nodding, like I’m listening, and then a few seconds later I realize I missed a key part and I have to ask them to repeat it. Which is embarrassing, because it looks like I wasn’t paying attention even though I thought I was.\n\nOutside work, maybe it happens in conversations too, but I can’t think of a super clean example right now.\n\nDoctor: When you're driving, do you notice similar issues—like feeling restless, making impulsive decisions (such as cutting people off or changing lanes quickly), or losing focus and missing turns or exits?\n\nPatient: Driving… I don’t know, I’m not sure I’d say that’s the main place I notice it. I don’t really have a clear example of cutting people off or anything like that.\n\nThe thing it reminds me of more is when I’m in longer strategy meetings, or even reading a client brief. I’ll start off focused, like I’m genuinely trying to follow it, and then a few minutes later I realize I’ve been staring at the same slide or the same paragraph and I don’t actually know what the last point was. Then I have to kind of backtrack mentally, or reread it.\n\nSo with driving, I’m not sure. Maybe it’s not as obvious there. But that “I was looking at it and somehow didn’t take it in” thing definitely happens with meetings and briefs.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been told by a partner, roommate, or family member that you seem distracted, restless, or forget things around the house—like household responsibilities or commitments you've made to them?\n\nPatient: I mean… not so much around the house that I can give you a perfect example. My partner has said I seem like I “leave things unfinished,” but when I think of the clearest version of that, it’s honestly more work stuff again.\n\nLike I’ll do almost all of a proposal or a CRM update, and in my head it feels basically done, but then I leave off the last step—sending the final version, or logging the follow-up. And that’s the part that actually matters. I meant to finish it, I didn’t decide not to, I just sort of move on mentally before I close the loop.\n\nAt home it’s probably similar with little responsibilities, but I’m having trouble naming one clean example.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Which is annoying because some of those briefs are already kind of dense and full of buzzwords, and then I start thinking about how we’re going to pitch it, or what the client really means by “brand authenticity,” and then I realize I’ve completely stopped absorbing the actual text.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others, blurting out responses before someone finishes speaking, or having trouble waiting your turn in conversations or meetings?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do that. I’ll jump in with an answer before the person’s even finished the question—especially with clients or coworkers—because I think I know where they’re going with it. And then two seconds later I realize, oh, that wasn’t actually what they were asking, or I kind of cut them off.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, honestly. Sometimes in meetings I’m trying to look engaged and quick, and then I end up answering the wrong thing because I didn’t let them finish.\n\nDoctor: Do you notice this same kind of restlessness, distractibility, or impulsivity in other parts of your life outside of work—like at home, during conversations with friends or family, or when you're doing personal tasks?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I probably do sometimes, but I haven’t really tracked it outside of work the same way. Work is just where it’s most obvious because there are meetings and people notice.\n\nAt home it’s more like… I’ll start doing something and then end up doing three other little things, but I don’t know if that’s the same thing or just normal. And with friends, I try not to interrupt, though I’m sure I’ve done it. Sorry, I’m kind of thinking of random examples now. I guess I’d say I haven’t noticed it as clearly outside work.\n\nDoctor: Thinking specifically about home—like paying bills, managing chores, or following through on personal projects—do you find yourself losing focus, leaving tasks unfinished, or feeling restless in the same way you do at work?\n\nPatient: At home, I’m honestly not sure I can give a clean example. I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nThe clearest version I notice is still with work stuff, like I’ll get almost all of a proposal done or update most of something in the CRM, and then somehow leave off the very last step—sending the final version, or logging the follow-up—even though I fully meant to finish it. It’s not like I decided not to do it, it’s more like I mentally moved on too early.\n\nSo… at home, maybe it happens, but I’m having a harder time pinning down a specific example. The follow-through issue is definitely easier for me to see with work tasks.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me about a specific example of paying bills or handling personal paperwork at home—do you ever miss due dates, forget to finish the process, or lose track of the task partway through?\n\nPatient: With bills or personal paperwork, I’m trying to think of a really specific example and I’m blanking a little. I don’t want to say I’m constantly missing due dates if I’m not sure.\n\nThe thing that’s most like that, though, is at work—like I’ll get almost all the way through a proposal or a CRM update, and then somehow not do the final step. Like I’ll forget to send the final version, or I won’t log the follow-up even though that was the whole point. And it’s not that I didn’t care or decided to skip it, I just kind of mentally move on before it’s actually finished.\n\nAt home, I guess it might happen with paperwork too, but I’m having trouble pulling up a clean example right now.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work, do you notice these issues affecting things like your social life or hobbies—for example, losing focus during conversations, forgetting plans, or feeling restless during activities you enjoy, like watching a movie or being at a social gathering?\n\nPatient: With social stuff, I’m not sure I notice it as much as at work. I mean, if I’m with friends I can usually follow along, unless it’s a loud place or something, but that feels kind of normal.\n\nThe clearer thing is when someone is talking directly to me at work—like my manager giving me feedback, or a client explaining what they want changed. I’ll be looking right at them, nodding, like I’m listening, and then a few seconds later I realize I missed a key part and I have to ask them to repeat it. Which is embarrassing, because it looks like I wasn’t paying attention even though I thought I was.\n\nOutside work, maybe it happens in conversations too, but I can’t think of a super clean example right now.\n\nDoctor: When you're driving, do you notice similar issues—like feeling restless, making impulsive decisions (such as cutting people off or changing lanes quickly), or losing focus and missing turns or exits?\n\nPatient: Driving… I don’t know, I’m not sure I’d say that’s the main place I notice it. I don’t really have a clear example of cutting people off or anything like that.\n\nThe thing it reminds me of more is when I’m in longer strategy meetings, or even reading a client brief. I’ll start off focused, like I’m genuinely trying to follow it, and then a few minutes later I realize I’ve been staring at the same slide or the same paragraph and I don’t actually know what the last point was. Then I have to kind of backtrack mentally, or reread it.\n\nSo with driving, I’m not sure. Maybe it’s not as obvious there. And that’s the part that actually matters. I meant to finish it, I didn’t decide not to, I just sort of move on mentally before I close the loop.\n\nAt home it’s probably similar with little responsibilities, but I’m having trouble naming one clean example.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work, do you notice yourself fidgeting, tapping your leg, talking more than others, or feeling like you need to keep moving—for example, during a long dinner, a movie, or just relaxing at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I don’t know if I’d say I’m super fidgety at home during a movie or dinner. And then I worry it looks rude or like I’m not taking the meeting seriously, even though I am trying to.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a specific hobby or personal project—like reading a book, doing a home repair, or something similar—where you've noticed yourself losing focus, leaving it unfinished, or feeling restless in the middle of it?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’m trying to think of a hobby example, and I’m sort of blanking on a clean one. I meant to finish it, I didn’t decide not to, I just sort of move on mentally before I close the loop.\n\nAt home it’s probably similar with little responsibilities, but I’m having trouble naming one clean example.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work, do you notice yourself fidgeting, tapping your leg, talking more than others, or feeling like you need to keep moving—for example, during a long dinner, a movie, or just relaxing at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I don’t know if I’d say I’m super fidgety at home during a movie or dinner. Maybe a little, but that’s not the thing that made me think, “Okay, this is a problem.”\n\nHonestly, what made me come in is more that in long sales strategy meetings, I keep getting up when I know I’m supposed to stay seated. Like I’ll tell myself I’m just refilling my water, or I need to check something by the printer, but really I think I just can’t sit there the whole time. And then I worry it looks rude or like I’m not taking the meeting seriously, even though I am trying to.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a specific hobby or personal project—like reading a book, doing a home repair, or something similar—where you've noticed yourself losing focus, leaving it unfinished, or feeling restless in the middle of it?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’m trying to think of a hobby example, and I’m sort of blanking on a clean one. Like I’ll start a book sometimes and then suddenly realize I’ve reread the same page, but the place I really notice it is still more with work reading.\n\nLike with a client brief, I’ll sit down meaning to really absorb it, and I’m looking right at the paragraph, but a few minutes later I realize I have no idea what I just read. Same with strategy meetings — I’ll be staring at the slide, nodding along, and then I’m like, wait, what was the last point? Then I have to go back or ask myself what I missed, which is kind of embarrassing.\n\n[Last Question]\nThinking about just the past week, can you recall a specific non-work task—like paying a bill, replying to a personal text, or finishing a chore—that you started but didn't complete?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 30 / advertising_sales_agent / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Instruction_Follow through_Difficulty\nmanifestation: \"I’ll get most of a proposal or CRM update done, but then I leave off the last step, like sending the final version or logging the follow-up, even though I meant to finish it.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
This past week… I’m trying to think of a non-work one, like an actual bill or text, but the clearest thing that comes to mind is still work-adjacent. Sorry, I know you asked non-work.

It’s that same pattern where I’ll get almost all of something done, like a proposal or a CRM update, and then somehow I don’t do the final step. Like I’ll have the proposal basically ready but not send the final version, or I’ll mean to log the follow-up and then just… don’t close it out. In my head it feels finished, even though technically the important last piece isn’t done.

It sounds kind of silly saying it out loud, because everyone gets restless, right? But it’s been noticeable enough that I thought maybe I should talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed this kind of restlessness and difficulty sitting still since you were a child, or is this something that started more recently as an adult?\n\nPatient: No, it definitely goes back. I mean, I don’t remember thinking of it as “restlessness” back then, but in elementary school my teachers would write stuff like I was bright but rushed through things, talked out of turn, forgot homework… and my desk was always this disaster, like stuffed with half-finished papers. My mom used to make me clean it out and I’d find worksheets from weeks before. So, yeah, it wasn’t just something that suddenly started with work.\n\nDoctor: When you notice yourself needing to get up and move during meetings, is it usually accompanied by a lot of worried or anxious thoughts running through your mind, or does it happen even when you're not particularly worried about anything?\n\nPatient: It happens even when I’m not especially worried. Like, the big thing is those long sales strategy meetings—I know I’m supposed to stay seated, but I’ll just feel like I have to get up, refill my water, or I’ll wander over by the printer and kind of pace for a minute. Like I’ll forget to send the final version, or I won’t log the follow-up even though that was the whole point. And it’s not that I didn’t care or decided to skip it, I just kind of mentally move on before it’s actually finished.\n\nAt home, I guess it might happen with paperwork too, but I’m having trouble pulling up a clean example right now.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work, do you notice these issues affecting things like your social life or hobbies—for example, losing focus during conversations, forgetting plans, or feeling restless during activities you enjoy, like watching a movie or being at a social gathering?\n\nPatient: With social stuff, I’m not sure I notice it as much as at work. I mean, if I’m with friends I can usually follow along, unless it’s a loud place or something, but that feels kind of normal.\n\nThe clearer thing is when someone is talking directly to me at work—like my manager giving me feedback, or a client explaining what they want changed. Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli. Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nI guess the main thing is work meetings.   }

Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. Subtypes: Childlike Silliness, Stupor, Catalepsy, Waxy Flexibility, Mutism, Negativism, Posturing, Mannerism, Stereotypy, Agitation, Grimacing, Echolalia, Echopraxia\n[S023] Negative_Symptoms: A state where emotions, motivation, speech, or social interest that should normally be present are diminished or lost.  Subtypes: Diminished Emotional Expression, Avolition, Alogia, Anhedonia, Asociality\n[S024] Depressed_Mood: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.  Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).  Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).  Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nIt happens even when I’m not especially worried. Like, the big thing is those long sales strategy meetings—I know I’m supposed to stay seated, but I’ll just feel like I have to get up, refill my water, or I’ll wander over by the printer and kind of pace for a minute. Sometimes I’m thinking about the meeting, sometimes I’m not thinking about much at all. It’s not always, like, anxiety driving it.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"
  }
]

Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\n{\"key\":\"S002\",\"name\":\"Sustained_Attention_Difficulty\",\"description\":\"Often has difficulty sustaining attention in tasks or play activities.\",\"reason\":\"The doctor is asking about current inattention symptoms, especially losing focus during tasks.\"}\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [judge] ==========

Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event. Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others. Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event. Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world. Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others. Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli. Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep. The follow-through issue is definitely easier for me to see with work tasks.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [judge] ==========
{"confirmed":["S004"],"denied":[],"reasoning":{"S004":"The patient describes repeatedly completing most of work tasks but failing to finish the final required step, such as sending the final version or logging follow-up, despite intending to complete it."}}

Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nWith bills or personal paperwork, I’m trying to think of a really specific example and I’m blanking a little.   }

Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort. Subtypes: Diminished Emotional Expression, Avolition, Alogia, Anhedonia, Asociality\n[S024] Depressed_Mood: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.  Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).  Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).  Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others. Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\n{\"key\":\"S004\",\"name\":\"Instruction_Follow through_Difficulty\",\"description\":\"Often does not follow through on instructions and fails to finish schoolwork, chores, or duties in the workplace.\",\"reason\":\"The question is most directly about forgetting or not completing household responsibilities or commitments, which matches difficulty following through on tasks or duties.\"}\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate.   }
